What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what finally got me to come in is I keep checking things—boxes, lists, drawers in the condo—over and over again before I can move on. I know I’ve already looked, that’s the foolish part, but I still feel like I have to do it again.\n\nMy wife will be standing there waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I’ll say, “Just a minute,” and then somehow I’m back at the same drawer again. It’s not as if anything new is going to appear there, you know, but I can’t quite let it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way exactly. It’s more that I can’t seem to move on until I’ve checked again—the same boxes, the same little lists, the drawers in the condo. I know I already looked, perfectly well, and my wife will be waiting there, sort of sighing at me to just leave it be.\n\nIt’s funny, we used to keep old tax papers in one of those drawers, and I suppose drawers always feel like they’re hiding something, but no, I don’t think I’m imagining some disaster so much as I just feel stuck until I look one more time.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that, I suppose. It’s not like a voice or anything dramatic, but these nagging thoughts keep circling back—like maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool in the wrong box.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Well, that’s not likely, you would’ve noticed,” but then it comes back anyway. I used to keep very organized files when I was teaching—probably too organized, my wife would say—and now if I can’t place something in my mind, it’s hard to settle. I’ll try to think about something else, and there it is again, bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've checked these things?\n\nPatient: A little, I suppose, but it’s not as satisfying as you’d think. It’s more that I can move on for the moment after I’ve checked the box or the list or the condo drawer again.\n\nI know perfectly well I already looked, and my wife will be waiting there for me to just leave it be, but until I do it, I feel sort of stuck. Then after I check, there’s a bit of relief, yes… though I feel rather foolish about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Not exactly a special order, no… or at least I don’t think of it that way. It’s more that I get caught checking the same boxes, the same lists, those drawers in the condo, before I can let myself move on.\n\nI know I’ve already looked—perfectly well, really—and my wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone. But somehow I end up going back over them again. It’s not a ritual like, say, left to right or anything… though now that you ask, I suppose I do tend to repeat the same little route without meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you repeat these checks?\n\nPatient: Not something dramatic, no. It’s not as if I think the building will burn down or anything like that. It’s more… I can’t quite move on until I’ve checked.\n\nThe thing that brought me in is I keep going back to the same boxes, the same lists, the condo drawers—over and over, really—even though I know perfectly well I already looked. My wife will be standing there waiting for me to leave it be, and I know she’s right, but I still feel I have to make sure before I can settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be arranged or done in a perfectly symmetrical or exact way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, I like things tidy enough—my wife would probably laugh at that, because my desk at home has old papers in little piles, and I always say I know where everything is, though half the time I don’t.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed needing things perfectly even or symmetrical. It’s more just… well, I’m not sure that’s the part that troubles me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks help you regain a sense of control over your environment?\n\nPatient: I suppose in a way, yes… though I don’t think of it as “control,” exactly. It’s more like I can’t move on until I’ve looked again.\n\nIt’s those same boxes and lists, and the drawers at the condo—I’ll check them, and then I know I’ve checked them, but somehow I’m back there again. My wife will be waiting, you know, with that look like, “Can we please go now?” and I know she’s right. I do. But if I don’t go back over it, I feel unsettled, like I haven’t quite finished something.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that if you don't check these things, you might make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s closer to it. It’s not that I imagine some disaster, it’s more this nagging thought that I may have made a mistake—thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool in the wrong box.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, “No, that’s probably not what happened,” but then it comes back again. Those teaching files, you know, some of them go back years, and half of them I don’t even need anymore, but then I think, well, what if there was something in there I did need? So I go back and look, and even after I’ve looked, it’s hard to feel settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the reason you keep checking is because you're afraid of overlooking something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it—overlooking something. It’s this little thought that keeps poking at me, like maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool I’ll need into the wrong box.\n\nAnd I’ll say to myself, “Oh, come on, that’s probably not true,” but then I’m thinking about it again. Those old files, you know, some of them I carried from one office to another for years, and half the time I can’t even remember why I kept them. But still, once the thought gets in there, it’s hard to settle unless I check.\n\nDoctor: Do you trust your memory when you've already checked something, or do you worry that you might have missed something even after you've looked?\n\nPatient: I know I’ve already checked, that’s the odd part. I can stand there and remember opening the drawer or looking through the box, and still I’ll think, “Well, did I really see everything?”\n\nIt’s been happening with those boxes, the lists, and the drawers at the condo. Before I can leave, I’ll go back over them again, even though my wife is waiting and I know she just wants me to let it be. And she’s not wrong. I just have trouble moving on until I’ve looked one more time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the reason you keep checking is because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it is that—making a mistake, or thinking I might have. It’s not like I’m picturing some terrible thing happening, exactly. It’s more this nagging thought that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I still need into the wrong box.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, “Well, that’s unlikely,” but then the thought comes back. Those files, you know, I kept them for years from when I was teaching, and some of them I probably should’ve tossed long ago, but then I start wondering what was in them. So it’s hard to feel settled unless I check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be perfect or exactly right before you can stop checking them?\n\nPatient: Perfect, I don’t know if I’d call it perfect exactly. It’s more that I can’t quite move on until it feels settled, if that makes sense.\n\nWhat brought me in is really that I keep checking the same boxes, the lists, the drawers at the condo—over and over, even though I know perfectly well I already looked. And my wife will be standing there waiting, ready to go, and I’m still thinking, “Let me just check that one drawer again.”\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I was teaching and I’d go back over papers or folders before leaving the office, though this is more bothersome now because there’s no real reason for it. I know I’ve checked. I just have trouble leaving it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to find everything in its exact place before you can stop checking?\n\nPatient: Not so much that everything has to be in its exact place, no. My wife will be standing there waiting for me to leave it be, and I know she’s right, but I still feel I have to make sure before I can settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be arranged or done in a perfectly symmetrical or exact way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, I like things tidy enough—my wife would probably laugh at that, because my desk at home has old papers in little piles, and I always say I know where everything is, though half the time I don’t.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed needing things perfectly even or symmetrical. It’s more just… well, I’m not sure that’s the part that troubles me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks help you regain a sense of control over your environment?\n\nPatient: I suppose in a way, yes… though I don’t think of it as “control,” exactly. It’s more like I can’t move on until I’ve looked again.\n\nIt’s those same boxes and lists, and the drawers at the condo—I’ll check them, and then I know I’ve checked them, but somehow I’m back there again. My wife will be waiting, you know, with that look like, “Can we please go now?” and I know she’s right. I do. But if I don’t go back over it, I feel unsettled, like I haven’t quite finished something.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that if you don't check these things, you might make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s closer to it. It’s not that I imagine some disaster, it’s more this nagging thought that I may have made a mistake—thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool in the wrong box.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, “No, that’s probably not what happened,” but then it comes back again. Those teaching files, you know, some of them go back years, and half of them I don’t even need anymore, but then I think, well, what if there was something in there I did need? So I go back and look, and even after I’ve looked, it’s hard to feel settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the reason you keep checking is because you're afraid of overlooking something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it—overlooking something. It’s this little thought that keeps poking at me, like maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool I’ll need into the wrong box.\n\nAnd I’ll say to myself, “Oh, come on, that’s probably not true,” but then I’m thinking about it again. Those old files, you know, some of them I carried from one office to another for years, and half the time I can’t even remember why I kept them. But still, once the thought gets in there, it’s hard to settle unless I check.\n\nDoctor: Do you trust your memory when you've already checked something, or do you worry that you might have missed something even after you've looked?\n\nPatient: I know I’ve already checked, that’s the odd part. I can stand there and remember opening the drawer or looking through the box, and still I’ll think, “Well, did I really see everything?”\n\nIt’s been happening with those boxes, the lists, and the drawers at the condo. Before I can leave, I’ll go back over them again, even though my wife is waiting and I know she just wants me to let it be. And she’s not wrong. I just have trouble moving on until I’ve looked one more time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the reason you keep checking is because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it is that—making a mistake, or thinking I might have. It’s not like I’m picturing some terrible thing happening, exactly. It’s more this nagging thought that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I still need into the wrong box.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, “Well, that’s unlikely,” but then the thought comes back. Those files, you know, I kept them for years from when I was teaching, and some of them I probably should’ve tossed long ago, but then I start wondering what was in them. So it’s hard to feel settled unless I check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be perfect or exactly right before you can stop checking them?\n\nPatient: Perfect, I don’t know if I’d call it perfect exactly. It’s more that I can’t quite move on until it feels settled, if that makes sense.\n\nWhat brought me in is really that I keep checking the same boxes, the lists, the drawers at the condo—over and over, even though I know perfectly well I already looked. And my wife will be standing there waiting, ready to go, and I’m still thinking, “Let me just check that one drawer again.”\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I was teaching and I’d go back over papers or folders before leaving the office, though this is more bothersome now because there’s no real reason for it. I know I’ve checked. I just have trouble leaving it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to find everything in its exact place before you can stop checking?\n\nPatient: Not so much that everything has to be in its exact place, no. I mean, I do like things put where I can find them—my wife would probably laugh at that, because I’ve had the same little habits for years—but that’s not really what gets me stuck.\n\nWhat brought me in is more that I’ll check the same boxes, the lists, and the drawers at the condo again and again before I can move on. I know I already looked. I can remember looking. But I’ll still think, “Just one more time,” and meanwhile my wife is waiting for me to leave it be.\n\nDoctor: Is it more important for you to make sure everything is correct when you check, or to feel certain that you haven't missed anything?\n\nPatient: It’s probably more the second one—feeling sure I haven’t missed something. I can look in a box and see what’s there, and still a little while later I’ll think, “But what if I threw out something from my old teaching files?” Those files sat around for years, you know, folders from classes and notes and things I used to think I’d need again.\n\nOr with the tools, I’ll think maybe I put one I still need in the wrong box. It isn’t so much that everything has to be correct in some perfect way. It’s that the thought keeps coming back, even after I tell myself, “Come on, that’s probably not the case.” Then I don’t feel settled, so I go check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking is more about avoiding mistakes or feeling in control of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more about avoiding a mistake, if I had to choose. Not a mistake like balancing a checkbook wrong—I used to be pretty good with records, actually, back when I was teaching—but more that nagging thought that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool I still need in the wrong box.\n\nAnd I can tell myself, “That’s probably not true, you already looked,” but the thought doesn’t quite leave. It comes back around, and then I don’t feel settled until I check again. So I suppose it’s not really about controlling the whole place, more about not being able to let that one possibility go.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly more about making sure you didn't make a mistake or about feeling absolutely certain that everything is okay?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I can’t move on until I’ve checked again, I suppose. I mean, I know perfectly well I already looked in the same boxes, or the condo drawers, or went over the list—sometimes more than once. But then I get this feeling like, “Well, just make sure,” and there I am doing it again.\n\nMy wife will be waiting for me, and I’ll know she’s waiting, and I’ll think of something else entirely for a second—like how we used to keep things in much better order when I had my office set up—but the point is, I keep going back to the same things. It’s less some grand feeling that everything is okay, and more that I can’t quite leave it be until I’ve checked one more time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

It’s probably the certainty part, though I suppose the mistake is in there too. What brought me in is I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, the condo drawers—over and over—before I can move on.

It comes back around, and then I don’t feel settled until I check again. So I suppose it’s not really about controlling the whole place, more about not being able to let that one possibility go.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly more about making sure you didn't make a mistake or about feeling absolutely certain that everything is okay?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I can’t move on until I’ve checked again, I suppose. I know I’ve already looked, that’s the foolish part, but I still feel like I have to do it again.\n\nMy wife will be standing there waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I’ll say, “Just a minute,” and then somehow I’m back at the same drawer again. It’s more that I can’t seem to move on until I’ve checked again—the same boxes, the same little lists, the drawers in the condo. I know I already looked, perfectly well, and my wife will be waiting there, sort of sighing at me to just leave it be.\n\nIt’s funny, we used to keep old tax papers in one of those drawers, and I suppose drawers always feel like they’re hiding something, but no, I don’t think I’m imagining some disaster so much as I just feel stuck until I look one more time.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that, I suppose. It’s not like a voice or anything dramatic, but these nagging thoughts keep circling back—like maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool in the wrong box.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Well, that’s not likely, you would’ve noticed,” but then it comes back anyway. I used to keep very organized files when I was teaching—probably too organized, my wife would say—and now if I can’t place something in my mind, it’s hard to settle. I’ll try to think about something else, and there it is again, bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've checked these things?\n\nPatient: A little, I suppose, but it’s not as satisfying as you’d think. It’s more that I can move on for the moment after I’ve checked the box or the list or the condo drawer again.\n\nI know perfectly well I already looked, and my wife will be waiting there for me to just leave it be, but until I do it, I feel sort of stuck. Then after I check, there’s a bit of relief, yes… though I feel rather foolish about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Not exactly a special order, no… or at least I don’t think of it that way. It’s more that I get caught checking the same boxes, the same lists, those drawers in the condo, before I can let myself move on.\n\nI know I’ve already looked—perfectly well, really—and my wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone. But somehow I end up going back over them again. It’s not a ritual like, say, left to right or anything… though now that you ask, I suppose I do tend to repeat the same little route without meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you repeat these checks?\n\nPatient: Not something dramatic, no. It’s not as if I think the building will burn down or anything like that. It’s more… I can’t quite move on until I’ve checked.\n\nThe thing that brought me in is I keep going back to the same boxes, the same lists, the condo drawers—over and over, really—even though I know perfectly well I already looked. My wife will be standing there waiting for me to leave it be, and I know she’s right, but I still feel I have to make sure before I can settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be arranged or done in a perfectly symmetrical or exact way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, I like things tidy enough—my wife would probably laugh at that, because my desk at home has old papers in little piles, and I always say I know where everything is, though half the time I don’t.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed needing things perfectly even or symmetrical. It’s more just… well, I’m not sure that’s the part that troubles me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks help you regain a sense of control over your environment?\n\nPatient: I suppose in a way, yes… though I don’t think of it as “control,” exactly. It’s more like I can’t move on until I’ve looked again.\n\nIt’s those same boxes and lists, and the drawers at the condo—I’ll check them, and then I know I’ve checked them, but somehow I’m back there again. My wife will be waiting, you know, with that look like, “Can we please go now?” and I know she’s right. I do. But if I don’t go back over it, I feel unsettled, like I haven’t quite finished something.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that if you don't check these things, you might make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s closer to it. It’s not that I imagine some disaster, it’s more this nagging thought that I may have made a mistake—thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool in the wrong box.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, “No, that’s probably not what happened,” but then it comes back again. Those teaching files, you know, some of them go back years, and half of them I don’t even need anymore, but then I think, well, what if there was something in there I did need? So I go back and look, and even after I’ve looked, it’s hard to feel settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the reason you keep checking is because you're afraid of overlooking something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it—overlooking something. It’s this little thought that keeps poking at me, like maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool I’ll need into the wrong box.\n\nAnd I’ll say to myself, “Oh, come on, that’s probably not true,” but then I’m thinking about it again. Those old files, you know, some of them I carried from one office to another for years, and half the time I can’t even remember why I kept them. But still, once the thought gets in there, it’s hard to settle unless I check.\n\nDoctor: Do you trust your memory when you've already checked something, or do you worry that you might have missed something even after you've looked?\n\nPatient: I know I’ve already checked, that’s the odd part. I can stand there and remember opening the drawer or looking through the box, and still I’ll think, “Well, did I really see everything?”\n\nIt’s been happening with those boxes, the lists, and the drawers at the condo. Before I can leave, I’ll go back over them again, even though my wife is waiting and I know she just wants me to let it be. And she’s not wrong. I just have trouble moving on until I’ve looked one more time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the reason you keep checking is because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it is that—making a mistake, or thinking I might have. It’s not like I’m picturing some terrible thing happening, exactly. It’s more this nagging thought that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I still need into the wrong box.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, “Well, that’s unlikely,” but then the thought comes back. Those files, you know, I kept them for years from when I was teaching, and some of them I probably should’ve tossed long ago, but then I start wondering what was in them. So it’s hard to feel settled unless I check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be perfect or exactly right before you can stop checking them?\n\nPatient: Perfect, I don’t know if I’d call it perfect exactly. It’s more that I can’t quite move on until it feels settled, if that makes sense.\n\nWhat brought me in is really that I keep checking the same boxes, the lists, the drawers at the condo—over and over, even though I know perfectly well I already looked. And my wife will be standing there waiting, ready to go, and I’m still thinking, “Let me just check that one drawer again.”\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I was teaching and I’d go back over papers or folders before leaving the office, though this is more bothersome now because there’s no real reason for it. I know I’ve checked. I just have trouble leaving it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to find everything in its exact place before you can stop checking?\n\nPatient: Not so much that everything has to be in its exact place, no. I mean, I do like things put where I can find them—my wife would probably laugh at that, because I’ve had the same little habits for years—but that’s not really what gets me stuck.\n\nWhat brought me in is more that I’ll check the same boxes, the lists, and the drawers at the condo again and again before I can move on. I know I already looked. I can remember looking. But I’ll still think, “Just one more time,” and meanwhile my wife is waiting for me to leave it be.\n\nDoctor: Is it more important for you to make sure everything is correct when you check, or to feel certain that you haven't missed anything?\n\nPatient: It’s probably more the second one—feeling sure I haven’t missed something. I can look in a box and see what’s there, and still a little while later I’ll think, “But what if I threw out something from my old teaching files?” Those files sat around for years, you know, folders from classes and notes and things I used to think I’d need again.\n\nOr with the tools, I’ll think maybe I put one I still need in the wrong box. It isn’t so much that everything has to be correct in some perfect way. It’s that the thought keeps coming back, even after I tell myself, “Come on, that’s probably not the case.” Then I don’t feel settled, so I go check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking is more about avoiding mistakes or feeling in control of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more about avoiding a mistake, if I had to choose. Not a mistake like balancing a checkbook wrong—I used to be pretty good with records, actually, back when I was teaching—but more that nagging thought that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool I still need in the wrong box.\n\nAnd I can tell myself, “That’s probably not true, you already looked,” but the thought doesn’t quite leave. It comes back around, and then I don’t feel settled until I check again. So I suppose it’s not really about controlling the whole place, more about not being able to let that one possibility go.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly more about making sure you didn't make a mistake or about feeling absolutely certain that everything is okay?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I can’t move on until I’ve checked again, I suppose. I mean, I know perfectly well I already looked in the same boxes, or the condo drawers, or went over the list—sometimes more than once. But then I get this feeling like, “Well, just make sure,” and there I am doing it again.\n\nMy wife will be waiting for me, and I’ll know she’s waiting, and I’ll think of something else entirely for a second—like how we used to keep things in much better order when I had my office set up—but the point is, I keep going back to the same things. It’s less some grand feeling that everything is okay, and more that I can’t quite leave it be until I’ve checked one more time.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly driven more by the fear of having made a mistake or by the need to feel completely certain about everything?\n\nPatient: It’s probably the certainty part, though I suppose the mistake is in there too. What brought me in is I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, the condo drawers—over and over—before I can move on.\n\nAnd the funny thing is, I know I already looked. I can remember looking. But then I’ll be standing there, and my wife is waiting for me to just leave it be, and I think, “Well, maybe just one more look.” It reminds me of when I used to keep papers for my classes, all those folders and notes—anyway, it’s that feeling that I can’t quite be sure enough to stop.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly driven by the fear of making a mistake or the need to feel certain that you haven't missed anything?\n\nPatient: It’s more the need to feel certain I haven’t missed something, I think. What brought me in is I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, the condo drawers—over and over—before I can move on.\n\nAnd I know I already looked, that’s the frustrating part. I can remember opening the drawer or going through the box, but then I’ll think, “Maybe I missed it.” My wife will be waiting for me to just leave it be, and I’ll still feel pulled back to look again. It’s not very dramatic, but it gets in the way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

But then I’ll be standing there, and my wife is waiting for me to just leave it be, and I think, “Well, maybe just one more look.” It reminds me of when I used to keep papers for my classes, all those folders and notes—anyway, it’s that feeling that I can’t quite be sure enough to stop.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly driven by the fear of making a mistake or the need to feel certain that you haven't missed anything?\n\nPatient: It’s more the need to feel certain I haven’t missed something, I think. What brought me in is I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, the condo drawers—over and over—before I can move on.\n\nAnd I know I already looked, that’s the frustrating part. I can remember opening the drawer or going through the box, but then I’ll think, “Maybe I missed it.” My wife will be waiting for me to just leave it be, and I’ll still feel pulled back to look again. It’s not very dramatic, but it gets in the way.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your need to check things repeatedly driven by the fear of making a mistake or the need to feel certain that everything is in order?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

It’s more that I need to feel everything is in order before I can step away. What brought me in is I keep checking the same boxes, the lists, the condo drawers, and then I check them again, even though I know perfectly well I already looked.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what finally got me to come in is I keep checking things—boxes, lists, drawers in the condo—over and over again before I can move on. I know I’ve already looked, that’s the foolish part, but I still feel like I have to do it again.\n\nMy wife will be standing there waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I’ll say, “Just a minute,” and then somehow I’m back at the same drawer again. It’s not as if anything new is going to appear there, you know, but I can’t quite let it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking these things to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way exactly. It’s more that I can’t seem to move on until I’ve checked again—the same boxes, the same little lists, the drawers in the condo. I know I already looked, perfectly well, and my wife will be waiting there, sort of sighing at me to just leave it be.\n\nIt’s funny, we used to keep old tax papers in one of those drawers, and I suppose drawers always feel like they’re hiding something, but no, I don’t think I’m imagining some disaster so much as I just feel stuck until I look one more time.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get that, I suppose. It’s not like a voice or anything dramatic, but these nagging thoughts keep circling back—like maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool in the wrong box.\n\nI’ll tell myself, “Well, that’s not likely, you would’ve noticed,” but then it comes back anyway. I used to keep very organized files when I was teaching—probably too organized, my wife would say—and now if I can’t place something in my mind, it’s hard to settle. I’ll try to think about something else, and there it is again, bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel less anxious after you've checked these things?\n\nPatient: A little, I suppose, but it’s not as satisfying as you’d think. It’s more that I can move on for the moment after I’ve checked the box or the list or the condo drawer again.\n\nI know perfectly well I already looked, and my wife will be waiting there for me to just leave it be, but until I do it, I feel sort of stuck. Then after I check, there’s a bit of relief, yes… though I feel rather foolish about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way or order?\n\nPatient: Not exactly a special order, no… or at least I don’t think of it that way. It’s more that I get caught checking the same boxes, the same lists, those drawers in the condo, before I can let myself move on.\n\nI know I’ve already looked—perfectly well, really—and my wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone. But somehow I end up going back over them again. It’s not a ritual like, say, left to right or anything… though now that you ask, I suppose I do tend to repeat the same little route without meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to prevent something specific from happening when you repeat these checks?\n\nPatient: Not something dramatic, no. It’s not as if I think the building will burn down or anything like that. It’s more… I can’t quite move on until I’ve checked.\n\nThe thing that brought me in is I keep going back to the same boxes, the same lists, the condo drawers—over and over, really—even though I know perfectly well I already looked. My wife will be standing there waiting for me to leave it be, and I know she’s right, but I still feel I have to make sure before I can settle.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be arranged or done in a perfectly symmetrical or exact way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, I like things tidy enough—my wife would probably laugh at that, because my desk at home has old papers in little piles, and I always say I know where everything is, though half the time I don’t.\n\nBut no, I haven’t really noticed needing things perfectly even or symmetrical. It’s more just… well, I’m not sure that’s the part that troubles me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checks help you regain a sense of control over your environment?\n\nPatient: I suppose in a way, yes… though I don’t think of it as “control,” exactly. It’s more like I can’t move on until I’ve looked again.\n\nIt’s those same boxes and lists, and the drawers at the condo—I’ll check them, and then I know I’ve checked them, but somehow I’m back there again. My wife will be waiting, you know, with that look like, “Can we please go now?” and I know she’s right. I do. But if I don’t go back over it, I feel unsettled, like I haven’t quite finished something.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that if you don't check these things, you might make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s closer to it. It’s not that I imagine some disaster, it’s more this nagging thought that I may have made a mistake—thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool in the wrong box.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, “No, that’s probably not what happened,” but then it comes back again. Those teaching files, you know, some of them go back years, and half of them I don’t even need anymore, but then I think, well, what if there was something in there I did need? So I go back and look, and even after I’ve looked, it’s hard to feel settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the reason you keep checking is because you're afraid of overlooking something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it—overlooking something. It’s this little thought that keeps poking at me, like maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool I’ll need into the wrong box.\n\nAnd I’ll say to myself, “Oh, come on, that’s probably not true,” but then I’m thinking about it again. Those old files, you know, some of them I carried from one office to another for years, and half the time I can’t even remember why I kept them. But still, once the thought gets in there, it’s hard to settle unless I check.\n\nDoctor: Do you trust your memory when you've already checked something, or do you worry that you might have missed something even after you've looked?\n\nPatient: I know I’ve already checked, that’s the odd part. I can stand there and remember opening the drawer or looking through the box, and still I’ll think, “Well, did I really see everything?”\n\nIt’s been happening with those boxes, the lists, and the drawers at the condo. Before I can leave, I’ll go back over them again, even though my wife is waiting and I know she just wants me to let it be. And she’s not wrong. I just have trouble moving on until I’ve looked one more time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the reason you keep checking is because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it is that—making a mistake, or thinking I might have. It’s not like I’m picturing some terrible thing happening, exactly. It’s more this nagging thought that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I still need into the wrong box.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, “Well, that’s unlikely,” but then the thought comes back. Those files, you know, I kept them for years from when I was teaching, and some of them I probably should’ve tossed long ago, but then I start wondering what was in them. So it’s hard to feel settled unless I check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be perfect or exactly right before you can stop checking them?\n\nPatient: Perfect, I don’t know if I’d call it perfect exactly. It’s more that I can’t quite move on until it feels settled, if that makes sense.\n\nWhat brought me in is really that I keep checking the same boxes, the lists, the drawers at the condo—over and over, even though I know perfectly well I already looked. And my wife will be standing there waiting, ready to go, and I’m still thinking, “Let me just check that one drawer again.”\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I was teaching and I’d go back over papers or folders before leaving the office, though this is more bothersome now because there’s no real reason for it. I know I’ve checked. I just have trouble leaving it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to find everything in its exact place before you can stop checking?\n\nPatient: Not so much that everything has to be in its exact place, no. I mean, I do like things put where I can find them—my wife would probably laugh at that, because I’ve had the same little habits for years—but that’s not really what gets me stuck.\n\nWhat brought me in is more that I’ll check the same boxes, the lists, and the drawers at the condo again and again before I can move on. I know I already looked. I can remember looking. But I’ll still think, “Just one more time,” and meanwhile my wife is waiting for me to leave it be.\n\nDoctor: Is it more important for you to make sure everything is correct when you check, or to feel certain that you haven't missed anything?\n\nPatient: It’s probably more the second one—feeling sure I haven’t missed something. I can look in a box and see what’s there, and still a little while later I’ll think, “But what if I threw out something from my old teaching files?” Those files sat around for years, you know, folders from classes and notes and things I used to think I’d need again.\n\nOr with the tools, I’ll think maybe I put one I still need in the wrong box. It isn’t so much that everything has to be correct in some perfect way. It’s that the thought keeps coming back, even after I tell myself, “Come on, that’s probably not the case.” Then I don’t feel settled, so I go check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking is more about avoiding mistakes or feeling in control of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more about avoiding a mistake, if I had to choose. Not a mistake like balancing a checkbook wrong—I used to be pretty good with records, actually, back when I was teaching—but more that nagging thought that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool I still need in the wrong box.\n\nAnd I can tell myself, “That’s probably not true, you already looked,” but the thought doesn’t quite leave. It comes back around, and then I don’t feel settled until I check again. So I suppose it’s not really about controlling the whole place, more about not being able to let that one possibility go.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly more about making sure you didn't make a mistake or about feeling absolutely certain that everything is okay?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I can’t move on until I’ve checked again, I suppose. It’s less some grand feeling that everything is okay, and more that I can’t quite leave it be until I’ve checked one more time.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly driven more by the fear of having made a mistake or by the need to feel completely certain about everything?\n\nPatient: It’s probably the certainty part, though I suppose the mistake is in there too. It’s more like I can’t move on until I’ve looked again.\n\nIt’s those same boxes and lists, and the drawers at the condo—I’ll check them, and then I know I’ve checked them, but somehow I’m back there again. My wife will be waiting, you know, with that look like, “Can we please go now?” and I know she’s right. I do. But if I don’t go back over it, I feel unsettled, like I haven’t quite finished something.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that if you don't check these things, you might make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s closer to it. It’s not that I imagine some disaster, it’s more this nagging thought that I may have made a mistake—thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool in the wrong box.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, “No, that’s probably not what happened,” but then it comes back again. Those teaching files, you know, some of them go back years, and half of them I don’t even need anymore, but then I think, well, what if there was something in there I did need? So I go back and look, and even after I’ve looked, it’s hard to feel settled.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the reason you keep checking is because you're afraid of overlooking something?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it—overlooking something. It’s this little thought that keeps poking at me, like maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool I’ll need into the wrong box.\n\nAnd I’ll say to myself, “Oh, come on, that’s probably not true,” but then I’m thinking about it again. Those old files, you know, some of them I carried from one office to another for years, and half the time I can’t even remember why I kept them. But still, once the thought gets in there, it’s hard to settle unless I check.\n\nDoctor: Do you trust your memory when you've already checked something, or do you worry that you might have missed something even after you've looked?\n\nPatient: I know I’ve already checked, that’s the odd part. I can stand there and remember opening the drawer or looking through the box, and still I’ll think, “Well, did I really see everything?”\n\nIt’s been happening with those boxes, the lists, and the drawers at the condo. Before I can leave, I’ll go back over them again, even though my wife is waiting and I know she just wants me to let it be. And she’s not wrong. I just have trouble moving on until I’ve looked one more time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the reason you keep checking is because you're afraid of making a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose it is that—making a mistake, or thinking I might have. It’s not like I’m picturing some terrible thing happening, exactly. It’s more this nagging thought that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I still need into the wrong box.\n\nAnd I’ll tell myself, “Well, that’s unlikely,” but then the thought comes back. Those files, you know, I kept them for years from when I was teaching, and some of them I probably should’ve tossed long ago, but then I start wondering what was in them. So it’s hard to feel settled unless I check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check need to be perfect or exactly right before you can stop checking them?\n\nPatient: Perfect, I don’t know if I’d call it perfect exactly. It’s more that I can’t quite move on until it feels settled, if that makes sense.\n\nWhat brought me in is really that I keep checking the same boxes, the lists, the drawers at the condo—over and over, even though I know perfectly well I already looked. And my wife will be standing there waiting, ready to go, and I’m still thinking, “Let me just check that one drawer again.”\n\nIt reminds me a little of when I was teaching and I’d go back over papers or folders before leaving the office, though this is more bothersome now because there’s no real reason for it. I know I’ve checked. I just have trouble leaving it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to find everything in its exact place before you can stop checking?\n\nPatient: Not so much that everything has to be in its exact place, no. I mean, I do like things put where I can find them—my wife would probably laugh at that, because I’ve had the same little habits for years—but that’s not really what gets me stuck.\n\nWhat brought me in is more that I’ll check the same boxes, the lists, and the drawers at the condo again and again before I can move on. I know I already looked. I can remember looking. But I’ll still think, “Just one more time,” and meanwhile my wife is waiting for me to leave it be.\n\nDoctor: Is it more important for you to make sure everything is correct when you check, or to feel certain that you haven't missed anything?\n\nPatient: It’s probably more the second one—feeling sure I haven’t missed something. I can look in a box and see what’s there, and still a little while later I’ll think, “But what if I threw out something from my old teaching files?” Those files sat around for years, you know, folders from classes and notes and things I used to think I’d need again.\n\nOr with the tools, I’ll think maybe I put one I still need in the wrong box. It isn’t so much that everything has to be correct in some perfect way. It’s that the thought keeps coming back, even after I tell myself, “Come on, that’s probably not the case.” Then I don’t feel settled, so I go check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your checking is more about avoiding mistakes or feeling in control of your surroundings?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more about avoiding a mistake, if I had to choose. Not a mistake like balancing a checkbook wrong—I used to be pretty good with records, actually, back when I was teaching—but more that nagging thought that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe I put a tool I still need in the wrong box.\n\nAnd I can tell myself, “That’s probably not true, you already looked,” but the thought doesn’t quite leave. It comes back around, and then I don’t feel settled until I check again. So I suppose it’s not really about controlling the whole place, more about not being able to let that one possibility go.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly more about making sure you didn't make a mistake or about feeling absolutely certain that everything is okay?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I can’t move on until I’ve checked again, I suppose. I mean, I know perfectly well I already looked in the same boxes, or the condo drawers, or went over the list—sometimes more than once. But then I get this feeling like, “Well, just make sure,” and there I am doing it again.\n\nMy wife will be waiting for me, and I’ll know she’s waiting, and I’ll think of something else entirely for a second—like how we used to keep things in much better order when I had my office set up—but the point is, I keep going back to the same things. It’s less some grand feeling that everything is okay, and more that I can’t quite leave it be until I’ve checked one more time.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly driven more by the fear of having made a mistake or by the need to feel completely certain about everything?\n\nPatient: It’s probably the certainty part, though I suppose the mistake is in there too. What brought me in is I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, the condo drawers—over and over—before I can move on.\n\nAnd the funny thing is, I know I already looked. I can remember looking. But then I’ll be standing there, and my wife is waiting for me to just leave it be, and I think, “Well, maybe just one more look.” It reminds me of when I used to keep papers for my classes, all those folders and notes—anyway, it’s that feeling that I can’t quite be sure enough to stop.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly driven by the fear of making a mistake or the need to feel certain that you haven't missed anything?\n\nPatient: It’s more the need to feel certain I haven’t missed something, I think. What brought me in is I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, the condo drawers—over and over—before I can move on.\n\nAnd I know I already looked, that’s the frustrating part. I can remember opening the drawer or going through the box, but then I’ll think, “Maybe I missed it.” My wife will be waiting for me to just leave it be, and I’ll still feel pulled back to look again. It’s not very dramatic, but it gets in the way.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly driven by the fear of making a mistake or the need to feel certain that everything is in order?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I need to feel everything is in order before I can step away. What brought me in is I keep checking the same boxes, the lists, the condo drawers, and then I check them again, even though I know perfectly well I already looked.\n\nMy wife will be waiting there for me to just leave it be, and I know she’s right, but I get stuck on it. I suppose years ago I liked things arranged a certain way too—papers, files, that sort of thing—but this is different. I just can’t quite move on until I’ve looked one more time."
